EXHIBIT A.1
92
Dear Members of the Planning Commission, 
We are pleased to present our proposal for the final commercial structure within the Quarry 
Village development — a thoughtfully designed shell building located on Lot 5. This building 
represents the culmination of the original master plan and reflects our commitment to high-
quality design, community integration, and long-term neighborhood value. 
Project Vision and Intent 
The Lot 5 building is a two-story, 13,728 square foot structure designed to accommodate a 
flexible mix of professional office and retail tenants. It is not configured to support restaurants, 
which aligns with both parking capacity and community vision. Our intent is to attract good-
credit tenants that provide useful services to the surrounding Pinebrook, Summit Park, and 
Jeremy Ranch neighborhoods, creating a cohesive and vibrant local center. 
Architectural Integration 
The building design is a modern interpretation of traditional Park City Main Street architecture, 
utilizing modules and materials that emphasize human scale and variety. Exterior finishes 
include wood siding, metal accents, and standing seam roofing, all carefully selected to visually 
integrate with the existing Quarry Village aesthetic. The north and south sides provide widened 
sidewalk zones and outdoor interaction areas, enhancing the pedestrian experience and 
supporting incidental gathering. 
Public Spaces and Plazas 
While this building does not propose a new standalone plaza, it benefits from and contributes to 
the existing central plaza space within Quarry Village. This shared plaza is maintained by the 
development and includes seating, landscaping, and community connectivity. Additionally, the 
Lot 5 building features two new outdoor plaza areas: an ADA-accessible space on the west side, 
and a rooftop plaza accessible via secure interior and exterior staircases. These will be primarily 
available to building tenants but can also serve as breakout areas for small community events. 
Functionality and Site Planning 
• Service Area: The trash enclosure and mechanical yard have been strategically located 
on the east side of the building, away from public view and adjacent properties. 
Screening is provided through architectural walls and landscaping. This area is accessed 
via the parking lot and is functionally and spatially adequate for tenant needs. 
• Snow Storage: Snow storage areas have been incorporated into landscape buffer zones 
along the edges of the property and meet or exceed the 10% requirement for uncovered 
parking areas. 
• Dual Front Design: The north and south façades are treated as primary elevations, each 
with multiple entries and storefront glazing. This dual-front approach reinforces 
pedestrian access and enhances flexibility for tenant configurations. 
EXHIBIT A.2
93
Compliance and Community Alignment 
This proposal fully complies with the Gorgoza Pines Consent Agreement and all applicable 
Summit County development standards. Specifically: 
• Height: Maximum height is 35 feet, compliant with the 2021 IBC and Consent 
Agreement allowances. 
• Scale: Designed for small-scale users with a traditional town layout. 
• Parking: Shared parking strategy consistent with the overall Quarry Village plan. 
• Trail & Circulation: New sidewalks and stairways improve circulation and pedestrian 
access within the broader development. 
 
We believe this building completes the Quarry Village development in a manner that strengthens 
its sense of place, supports local commercial vitality, and enhances neighborhood walkability. 
We welcome the opportunity to continue collaborating with staff and the Commission, and we 
respectfully ask for your support of this proposal. 
Deliveries 
The Quarry Village Lot 5 building has been intentionally designed without roll-up bay doors or 
dedicated loading docks. This reflects the anticipated tenant mix of professional offices and 
small-scale retailers who do not require frequent or large-volume bulk deliveries. The building is 
not intended to accommodate tenants with heavy delivery needs, such as warehousing, 
fulfillment centers, or logistics-driven uses. 
Should deliveries be required, they will occur at the front of each unit, utilizing the standard 
parking spaces directly adjacent to the tenant suites. These short-term deliveries can be managed 
without disruption to daily operations or parking availability, and the site has been designed with 
ample turning radii and curb cuts to accommodate small delivery vehicles and vans.

GLASS IN ALL DOOR SYSTEMS SHALL BE TEMPERED - TYP.. 
TEMPERED GLASS SHALL BE INSTALLED IN WINDOWS AS 
CALLED OUT ON FLOOR PLANS, WINDOW SCHEDULE OR AS 
SPECIFIED ON SHEET A0.1, TYP.

RAILING SYSTEM, NO OPENING TO ALLOW A 4" SPHERE TO 
PASS. REFER TO DETAIL 11 ON SHEET A6.0.
1. WRITTEN DIMENSIONS SHALL TAKE PRECEDENT OVER SCALED 
DIMENSIONS.
2. REFER TO SHEET A0.1 FOR PROJECT GENERAL NOTES AND 
NOTES PERTAINING TO; DECAY RESISTANT LUMBER, STUDS ON 
STEEL WORK, FIRE BLOCKING, ACCESS (Crawl Space & Attic), 
VENTILATION, MASONRY VENEERS, STAIRS, HANDRAILS, 
GUARDRAILS, SAFETY (Tempered) GLAZING, CAULKING, FLASHINGS 
AND SIDING INSTALLATIONS.
3. REFER TO SHEET A0.2 FOR AN OUTLINE PROJECT SPECIFICATION 
WHICH LISTS; MATERIALS, MOISTURE PROTECTION, 
WATERPROOFING, R & U VALUES, FINISHES, INSULATION VALUES 
AND VAPOR BARRIERS
4. WHEN THERE IS FOUND TO BE A DISCREPANCY IN THE 
CONSTRUCTION DOCUMENTS CONTACT THE ARCHITECT PRIOR TO 
PROCEEDING WITH ANY QUESTIONABLE WORK.

VENTILATION, MASONRY VENEERS, STAIRS, HANDRAILS, 
GUARDRAILS, SAFETY (Tempered) GLAZING, CAULKING, FLASHINGS 
AND SIDING INSTALLATIONS.
3. REFER TO SHEET A0.2 FOR AN OUTLINE PROJECT SPECIFICATION 
WHICH LISTS; MATERIALS, MOISTURE PROTECTION, 
WATERPROOFING, R & U VALUES, FINISHES, INSULATION VALUES 
AND VAPOR BARRIERS
4. WHEN THERE IS FOUND TO BE A DISCREPANCY IN THE 
CONSTRUCTION DOCUMENTS CONTACT THE ARCHITECT PRIOR TO 
PROCEEDING WITH ANY QUESTIONABLE WORK.
FLOOR PLAN GENERAL NOTES:
STAIR SYSTEM, REFER TO DETAILS AND NOTES PROVIDED 
ON SHEETS A4.1 & A6.1.

ENGINEERING DRWG'S.

LANDING A5.2
1
A5.2
2
NO ACCESS or STORAGE
UNDER STAIR SYSTEM
1011.2 STAIRWAY WIDTH.  THE WIDTH OF STAIRWAYS SHALL BE DETERMINED AS
SPECIFIED IN SECTION 1005.1, BUT SUCH WIDTH SHALL NOT BE LESS THAN 44 IN.. SEE
SECTION 1009.3 FOR ACCESSIBLE MEANS OF EGRESS STAIRWAYS.
EXCEPTIONS:
1011.3 HEADROOM. STAIRWAYS SHALL HAVE A  MINIMUM HEADROOM CLEARANCE OF 80 IN.
MEASURED VERTICALLY FROM A LINE CONNECTING THE EDGE OF THE NOSINGS. SUCH
HEADROOM SHALL BE CONTINUOUS ABOVE THE STAIRWAY TO THE POINT WHERE THE LINE
INTERSECTS THE LANDING BELOW, ONE TREAD DEPTH BEYOND THE BOTTOM RISER.  THE
MINIMUM CLEARANCE SHALL BE MAINTAINED THE FUL WIDTH OF THE STAIRWAY AND
LANDING.
1011.4 WALKLINE. THE WALKLINE ACROSS WINDER TREADS SHALL BE CONCENTRIC TO
THE DIRECTION OF TRAVEL THROUGH THE TURN AND LOCATED 12 IN. FROM THE SIDE
WHERE THE WINDERS ARE NARROWER, THE 12 IN. DIMENSION SHALL BE MEASURED
FROM THE WIDEST POINT OF THE CLEAR STAIR WIDTH AT THE WALKING SURFACE OF
THE WINDER. IF WINDERS ARE ADJACENT WITHIN THE FLIGHT, THE POINT OF THE
WIDEST CLEAR STAIR WIDTH OF ADJACENT WINDERS SHALL BE USED.
1011.5.2  RISERS HEIGHT AND TREAD DEPTH. STAIR RISER HEIGHTS SHALL BE 7 IN.
MAXIMUM AND 4 IN. MINIMUM.  THE RISER HEIGHT SHALL BE MEASURED VERTICALLY
BETW. THE LEADINGEDGES OF ADJACENT TREADS. RECTANGULAR TREAD DEPTHS
SHALL BE 11 IN. MIN. MEASURED HORIZONTALLY BETW. THE VERTICAL PLANES OF THE
FOREMOST PROJECTION OF ADJACENT TREADS AND AT A RIGHT ANGLE TO THE
TREAD'S LEADING EDGE. WINDER TREADS SHALL HAVE A MIN. TREAD DEPTH OF 11 IN.
MEASURED BETW. THE VERTICAL PLANES OF THE FOREMOST PROJECTION OF
ADJACENT TREADS AT THE INTERSECTIONS WITH THE WALKLINE AND A MIN. TREAD
DEPTH OF 10 IN. WITHUIN THE CLEAR WIDTH OF THE STAIR.
1011.5.3 WINDER TREADS. WINDER TREADS ARE NOT PERMITTED IN A MEANS OF
EGRESS STAIRWAYS EXCEPT WITHIN A DWELLING UNIT.
1011.5.4 DIMENSIONAL UNIFORMITY. STAIR TREADS AND RISERS SHALL BE OF
UNIFORM SIZE AND SHAPE.  THE TOLERANCE BETWEEN THE LARGEST AND SMALLEST
RISER HEIGHT OR BETW. THE LARGEST AND SMALLEST TREAD DEPTH SHALL NOT
EXCEED 0.375 IN. IN ANY FLIGHT OF STAIRS.  THE GREATEST WINDER TREAD DEPTH AT
THE WALKLINE WITHIN ANY FLIGHT OF STAIRS SHALL NOT EXCEED THE SMALLEST BY
MORE THAN 0.375 IN..
1011.5.5 NOSING AND RISER PROFILE.  NOSINGS SHALL HAVE A CURVATURE OR BEVEL
OF NOT LESS THAN 1/16-INCH BUT NOT MORE THAN 9/16-INCH FROM THE FOREMOST
PROJECTION OF TEH TREAD. RISERS SHALL BE SOLID AND VERTICAL OR SLOPED
UNDER THE TREAD ABOVE FROM THE UNDERSIDE OF THE NOSING ABOVE AT AN
ANGLE NOT MORE THAN 30 DEGREES FROM THE VERTICAL.
1011.5.5.1 NOSING PROJECTION SIZE. THE LEADING EDGE OF TREADS SHALL PROJECT
NOT MORE THAN 1.25 IN. BEYOND THE TREAD BELOW AND ALL PROJECTIONS OF THE
LEADING EDGES SHALL BE OF UNIFORM SIZE, INCLUDING THE LEADING EDGE OF THE
FLOOR AT THE TOP OF A FLIGHT.
1011.7  STAIRWAY CONSTRUCTION.   ALL STAIRWAYS SHALL BE BUILT OF MATERIALS
CONSISTENT WITH THE TYPES PERMITTED FOR THE TYPE OF CONSTRUCTION OF THE
BUILDING, EXCEPT THAT WOOD HANDRAILS SHALL BE PERMITTED FOR ALL TYPES OF
CONSTRUCTION.
1011.7.1 STAIRWAY WALKING SURFACE.   THE WALKING SURFACE OF TREADS AND
LANDINGS OF A STAIRWAY SHALL NOT BE SLOPED GREATER THAN ONE UNIT VERTICAL
IN 48 UNITS HIRIZONTAL (2 PERCENT SLOPE) IN ANY DIRECTION.  STAIRWAY TREADS
AND LANDINGS SHALL HAVE A SOLID SURFACE.  FINISH FLOOR SURFACES SHALL BE
SECURELY ATTACHED.
1011.7.2 OUTDOOR CONDITIONS.  OUTDOOR STAIRWAYS AND OUT DOOR APPROACHES
TO STAIRWAYS SHALL BE DESIGNED SO THAT WATER WILL NOT ACCUMULATE ON
WALKING SURFACES. REFERENCE CIVIL ENGINEERING DRWG'S.
1011.7.3 ENCLOSURES UNDER STAIRWAYS.  THE WALLS AND SOFFITS WITHIN
ENCLOSED USABLE SPACES UNDER ENCLOSED AND UNENCLOSED STAIRWAYS SHALL
BE PROTECTED BY 1-HOUR FIRE RESISTANCE CONSTRUCTION OF THE FIRE RATING
OF THE STAIRWAY ENCLOSURE, WHICHEVER IS GREATER.  ACCESS TO THE ENCLOSED
SPACE SHALL NOT BE DIRECTLY FROM WITHIN THE STAIR ENCLOSURE.
SECTION 1011 - STAIRWAYS
1. STAIRWAYS SERVING AN OCCUPANT LOAD OF LESS THAT 50 SHALL HAVE A WIDTH OF
NOT LESS THAN 36 IN.
EXCEPTIONS:
3. HANDRAIL BRACKETS OR BALUSTERS ATTACHED TO THE BOTTOM SURFACE OF THE
HANDRAIL THAT DO NOT PROJECT HORIZONTALLY BEYOND THE SIDES OF THE
HANDRAIL WITHIN 1.5-INCHES OF THE BOTTOM OF THE HANDRAIL SHALL NOT BE
CONSIDERED OBSTRUCTIONS.  FOR EACH 0.5 INCH OF ADDITIONAL HANDRAIL
PERIMETER DIMENSION ABOVE 4-INCHES THE VERTICAL CLEARANCE DIMENSION OF
1.5-INCHES SHALL BE PERMITTED TO BE REDUCED BY 0.125 - INCH.
SECTION 1014 - HANDRAILS
1011.8 VERTICAL RISE.  A FLIGHT OF STAIRS SHALL NOT HAVE A VERTICAL RISE GREATER
THAN 12 FEET BETWEEN LEVELS OR LANDINGS.
1011.11 HANDRAILS.  STAIRWAYS SHALL HAVE HANDRAILS ON EACH SIDE AND SHALL
COMPLY WITH SECTION 1014.
1014.1 WHERE REQUIRED.  HANDRAILS REQUIRED FOR STAIRWAYS SHALL BE ADEQUATE IN
STRENGTH AND ATTACHEMENT IN ACCORDANCE WITH SECTION 1607.8. HANDRAILS
REQUIRED FOR STAIRWAYS BY SECTION 1011.11 SHALL COMPLY WITH SECTIONS 1014.2
THROUGH 1014.9.
1014.2 HEIGHT.  HANDRAIL HEIGHT, MEASURE ABOVE STAIR TREAD NOSINGS SHALL BE
UNIFORM, NOT LESS THAN 34-INCHES AND NOT MORE THAN 38-INCHES
1014.3 HANDRAIL GRASPABILITY.  ALL REQUIRED HANDRAILS SHALL COMPLY WITH
SECTION 1012.3.1 OR SHALL PROVIDE EQUIVALENT GRASPABILITY.
1014.3.1 TYPE I. HANDRAILS WITH A CIRCULR CROSS-SECTION SHALL HAVE AN OUTSIDE
DIAMETER OF A LEAST 1.25-INCHES AND NOT GREATER THAN 2-INCHES.  WHERE THE
HANDRAIL IS NOT CIRCULAR, IT SHALL HAVE A PERIMTER DIMESNION OF AT LEAST 4-
INCHES AND NOT GREATER THAN 6.25-INCHES WITH A MAX. CROSS-SECTION DIMENSION
OF 2.25-INCHES. EDGES SHALL HAVE A MIN. RADIUS OF 0.01-INCH.
1014.4 CONTINUITY.  HANDRAIL GRIPPING SURFACES SHALL BE CONTINUOUS, WITHOUT
INTERRUPTION BY NEWEL POSTS OR OTHER OBSTRUCTIONS.
SECTION 1015 - GUARDS
1015.2 WHERE REQUIRED.  GAURDS SHALL BE LOCATED ALONG OPEN-SIDED WALKING
SURFACE, INCLUDING MEZZANINES, EQUIPMENT PLATFORMS, STAIRS, RAMPS, AND
LANDINGS THAT ARE LOCATED MORE THAN 30-INCHES MEASURED VERTICALLY TO THE
FLOOR OR GRADE BELOW AT ANY POINT WITHIN 36-INCHES HORIZONTALLY TO THE EDGE
OF THE OPEN SIDE. GUARDS SHALL BE ADEQUATE IN STRENGTH AND THE ATTACHMENT IN
ACCORDANCE WITH SECTION 1607.8.
1015.3 HEIGHT.  REQUIRED GUARDS SHALL BE NOT LESS THAN 42-INCHES HIGH,
MEASURED VERTICALLY ABOVE THE ADJACENT WALKING SURFACES, ADJACENT FIXED
SEATING OR THE LINE CONNECTING THE LEADING EDGES OF THE TREADS.
1015.4 OPENING LIMITATIONS.  REQUIRED GUARDS SHALL NOT HAVE OPENINGS WHICH
ALLOW PASSAGE OF A SPHERE 4-INCH DIA. FROM THE WALKING SURFACE TO THE
REQUIRED GUARD HEIGHT.
1014.5 FITTINGS. HANDRAILS SHALL NOT ROTATE WITHIN THEIR FITTINGS.
1014.6 HANDRAIL EXTENSIONS.  HANDRAILS SHALL RETURN TO A WALL, GUARD OR THE
WALKING SURFACE OR SHALL BE CONTINUOUS TO THE HANDRAIL OF AN ADJACENT STAIR
FLIGHT OR RAMP RUN.  WHERE HANDRAILS ARE NOT CONTINUOUS BETWEEN FLIGHTS,
THE HANDRAILS SHALL EXTEND HORIZONTALLY AT LEAST 12-INCHES BEYOND THE TOP
RISER AND CONTINUE TO SLOPE FOR THE DEPTH OF ONE TREAD BEYOND THE BOTTOM
RISER.  THE EXTENSIONS OF HANDRAILS SHALL BE IN THE SAME DIRECTION OF THE STAIR
FLIGHTS AT STAIRWAYS.
1014.7 CLEARANCE.  CLEAR SPACE BETW. A HANDRAIL AND A WALL OR OTHER SURFACE
SHALL BE A MIN. OF 1.25-INCHES. A HANDRAIL AND A WALL OR OTHER SURFACE ADJACENT
TO THE HANDRAIL SHALL BE FREE OF ANY SHARP OR ABRASIVE ELEMENTS.
1014.8 PROJECTIONS.  PROJECTIONS INTO THE REQUIRED WIDTH OF STAIRWAYS AT EACH
HANDRAIL SHALL NOT EXCEED 4.25-INCHES AT OR BELOW THE HANDRAIL HEIGHT.
PROJECTIONS INTO THE REQUIRED WIDTH SHALL NOT BE LIMITED ABOVE THE MIN.
HEADROOM HEIGHT REQUIRED IN SECTION 1011.3.
1014.9 INTERMEDIATE HANDRAILS.  STAIRWAYS SHALL HAVE INTERMEDIATE HANDRAILS
LOCATED IN SUCH A MANNER THAT ALL PORTIONS OF THE STAIRWAY WIDTH REQUIRED
FOR EGRESS CAPACITY ARE WITHIN 30-INCHES OF A HANDRAIL.
1011.6 STAIRWAY LANDINGS.  THERE SHALL BE A FLOOR OR LANDING AT THE TOP AND
BOTTOM OF EACH STAIRWAY.  THE WIDTH OF LANDINGS SHALL NOT BE LESS THAN
THE WIDTH OF STAIRWAYS THEY SERVE.  EVERY LANDING SHALL HAVE A MINUMUM
DIMENSION MEASURED IN THE DIRECTION OF TRAVEL EQUAL TO THE WIDTH OF THE
STAIRWAY.  SUCH DIMENSION NEED NOT EXEED 48 IN. WHERE THE STAIRWAY HAS A
STRAIGHT RUN.  DOORS OPENING ONTO A LANDING SHALL NOT REDUCE THE LANDING
TO LESS THA ONE-HALF THE REQUIRED WIDTH.  WHEN FULLY OPEN, THE DOOR SHALL
NOT PROJECT MORE THAN 7 IN. INTO A LANDING. WHEN WHEELCHAIR SPACES ARE
REQUIRED ON THE STAIRWAY LANDING IN ACCORDANCE WITH SECTION 1007.6.1 THE
WHEELCHAIR SPACE SHALL NOT BE LOCATED IN THE REQUIRED WIDTH OF THE
LANDING AND DOORS SHALL NOT SWING OVER THE WHEELCHAIR SPACES.

LANDSCAPING INSTALLATIONS
INSTALLATION: 
1. IT SHALL BE THE RESPONSIBILITY OF THE DEVELOPER TO GRADE, PLACE TOP SOIL, SEED OR 
SOD, INSTALL AUTOMATIC SPRINKLER IRRIGATION SYSTEMS, AND PROPERLY PLANT TREES, 
SHRUBS, AND OTHER APPROVED PLANT MATERIALS. 
2. ALL TREES PLANTED ON SITE LESS THAN TWO INCH (2") CALIPER SHALL BE DOUBLE STAKED.
3. ALL LANDSCAPING SHALL BE COMPLETED IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE LANDSCAPING PLAN, 
UNLESS OTHER WISE APPROVED BY THE ARCHITECT OF RECORD. 
 (A) ALL LANDSCAPE WORK MUST BE INSTALLED PRIOR TO A CERTIFICATE OF 
OCCUPANCY OF THE BUILDING BEING ISSUED OR AS OTHERWISE APPROVED THE SUMMIT 
COUNTY AS SEASONAL CONDITIONS MAY DICTATE.
 (B) THE DEVELOPER SHALL MEET ALL BONDING REQUIREMENTS FOR LANDSCAPE 
IMPROVEMENTS AS REQUIRED BY THE SUMMIT COUNTY. 
MAINTENANCE: 
1. IT SHALL BE THE RESPONSIBILITY OF THE PROPERTY OWNER TO PROPERLY MAINTAIN 
LANDSCAPED ARES IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE APPROVED SITE PLAN AND LANDSCAPE PLAN 
WHICH INCLUDES WATERING, MOWING, PROPER PRUNING, FERTILIZING, THE REMOVAL AND 
REPLACEMENT OF DEAD PLANT MATERIALS IN A TIMELY MANNER, AND THE MAINTENANCE OF THE 
IRRIGATION SYSTEMS TO ELIMINATE THE WASTE OF WATER. 
2. ALL PRUNNING SHALL BE ACCOMPLISHED ACCORDING TO GOOD HORTICULTURAL STANDARDS. 
TREES SHALL BE PRUNED TO PROMOTE HEALTHY TREES AND TO ACCOMMODATE STREET 
MAINTENANCE AND GARBAGE COLLECTION (MIN. HEIGHT OVER STREET 13.5 FEET AND OVER 
SIDEWALKS 7 FEET) AND AS NECESSARY TO PROMOTE HEALTHY GROWTH.
3. UNLESS APPROVAL IS OTHERWISE PROVIDED BY SUMMIT COUNTY, TREES SHALL BE ALLOWED 
TO ATTAIN THEIR NORMAL SIZE AND SHALL NOT BE SEVERLY PRUNED UP FROM THE GROUND OR 
"HAT RACKED" IN ORDER TO PERMANENTLY MAINTAIN GROWTH AT A REDUCED HEIGHT. 
PRUNNING TREES SOLELY FOR THE PUPOSE OF EXPOSURE IS PROHIBITED. 
4. PRUNING TREES FOR TRAFFIC SAFETY REASONS SHALL BE REVIEWED AND APPROVED BY THE 
LOCAL JURISDICATIONS TRAFFIC ENGINEER.  
LANDSCAPING GENERAL NOTES
GENERAL: 
1. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL LOCATED AND VERIFY THE EXISTANCE OF ALL UTILITIES PRIOR TO 
STARTING THE WORK.
2. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL SUPPLY ALL PLANT MATERIALS IN QUANTITIES SUFFICIENT TO 
COMPLETE THE PLANTINGS SHOWN ON THE DRAWINGS.
3. REFER TO WRITTEN SPECIFICATIONS FOR ADDITIONAL REQUIRED INFORMATION. 
4. ALL PLANTS SHALL BE SPRAYED WITH AN ANTI-DESCECANT WITHIN 24-HOURS AFTER 
PLANTING. ALL PLANTS SHALL BE SPRAYED WITH ANTI-DESECANT AT THE BEGINNING OF THEIR 
FIRST WINTER.
5. ALL PLANTS SHALL BE WATERED THOROUGHLY TWICE DURING THE FIRST 24-HOUR PERIOD 
AFTER PLANTING. ALL PLANTS SHALL THEN BE WATERED AS NEEDED AND SUGGESTED BY LOCAL 
CONDITIONS DURING THE FIRST GROWING SEASON.
6. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL REVIEW ARCHITECTURAL / ENGINEERING PLANS TO BECOME 
FAMILIAR WITH GRADING AND SUB-SURFACE UTILITIES.
7. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL INSURE THAT THEIR WORK DOES NOT INTERRUPT ESTABLISHED OR 
PROTECTED DRAINAGE PATTERNS.
8. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL IMMEDIATELY NOFITY THE ARCHITECT OF RECORD IF ROCK IS 
ENCOUNTERED WHILE DIGGING PLANTING HOLES FOR ANY TREES, SHRUB OR PLANT. THE 
CONTRACTOR SHALL NOT PLANT AND PLANT MATERIAL IN THESE HOLES WITHOUT APPROVAL OF 
THE ARCHTECT OF RECORD.
9. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL COORDINATE WITH LIGHTING AND IRRIGATION CONTRACTORS 
REGARDING TIMING OF INSTALLATION OF PLANT MATERIALS.
10. EVERY POSSIBLE SAFEGUARD SHALL BE TAKEN TO PROTECT BUILDING SURFACES, 
EQUIPMENT AND ON-GRADE HARD SURFACES. THE CONTRACTOR SHALL BE RESPONSIBLE FOR 
ANY DAMAGE OR INJURY TO PERSON OR PROPERTY WHICH MAY OCCUR AS A RESULT OF 
NEGLIGENCE ON THE PART OF THE CONTRACTOR IN THE EXECUTION OF THE WORK.
11. ALL PROPOSED PLANT MATERIALS SHALL BE INSTALLED EITHER ENTIRELY IN OR ENTIRELY 
OUT OF PLANTING BEDS. PLANTING BED LINES ARE NOT TO BE OBSTRUCTED.

STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Laura Kuhrmeyer, County Planner 
Date of Meeting: May 27, 2025 
Type of Item: Determination of Historic Significance & Conditional Use Permit (CUP) – 
Public Hearing, Possible Action 
Process:  Administrative Review 
Project Number:  25-062 
 
 
 
Project Description 
Project Name:   Hi Ute Barn 
Applicant(s):   Joseph Geroux 
Property Owner(s):  Utah Open Lands Conservation Association 
Location:   2201 W. Kilby Road 
Zone District:   Rural Residential (RR) and Hillside Stewardship (HS) 
Parcel Number and Size: Parcel #PP-52-1-A, 344.78 acres 
Type of Process:  Administrative 
Final Land Use Authority: Snyderville Basin Planning Commission  
  
Proposal 
The Applicant (Joseph Geroux), on behalf of the property owner (Utah Open Lands 
Conservation Association), is requesting that the Planning Commission make a determination of 
historic significance for the existing structure on Parcel PP-52-1-A, also known as the Hi Ute 
Barn. If the Planning Commission finds that the structure is historically significant, the Applicant 
also is requesting a Conditional Use Permit (CUP), as required in this zone by 10-2-10, for the 
Adaptive Reuse of a Historically Significant Structure.  
 
The Applicant proposes to make interior structural upgrades for life safety and preservation of 
the barn as well as some exterior restoration with in-kind replacement of existing windows, 
dilapidated siding, and new matching paint. The proposed adaptive reuse falls under the 
definition of “Cultural Activity” (see below). 
 
CULTURAL ACTIVITY: A facility providing nature exhibitions, libraries, museums, art 
galleries, botanical gardens, historic sites or similar uses displaying, preserving, and 
exhibiting objects, of community and cultural interest in one or more of the arts and 
sciences. 
 
112
Per Section 10-8-11 of the Snyderville Basin Development Code these uses can be approved 
through a Conditional Use permit, provided the structure is determined to have historic 
significance and the project includes rehabilitating the structure. 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
 
Background 
The Parcel (PP-52-1-A) is located partially in the Rural Residential (RR) Zone and partially in the 
Hillside Stewardship (HS) Zone, with the subject Structure located on the RR Zoned portion of 
the property. The Parcel is approximately 344.78 acres in size.  
 
The Hi Ute Barn was constructed in 1926, but the Hi Ute Ranch was homesteaded in 1876. The 
barn serves an iconic structure associated with agricultural, farming, and ranching in Summit 
County’s history. A collaboration of several conservation groups resulted in a Conservation 
Easement (Exhibit D) being placed on the parcel. The Conservation Easement anticipated a Use 
similar to the currently proposed Use (see image below). 
113
 
 
The crux of this proposal is the preservation and rehabilitation of the historic Structure. 
Because the applicant is proposing to preserve it, it is eligible for uses on the property that 
otherwise may not be available were it not Historic. Section 10-8-11 of the Snyderville Basin 
Development Code states:   
 
“The purpose of these regulations is to provide for the adaptive reuse of a historically 
significant building with a new use that meets the criteria set forth in this code.” 
 
The Structure was reviewed as part of the 2002 Reconnaissance Survey of Historic Buildings and 
Structures (Exhibit C). The Structure was found to be eligible/significant, though it is not 
currently listed on the National Register of Historic Places. 
 
Preservation  
The Applicant’s Written Description of the Proposal (Exhibit A), states that the following 
modifications are proposed: 
 
• Interior structural upgrades for life, safety, and preservation of the barn (with respect to 
seismic events) 
• Exterior changes are limited to restoring the Structure with: 
o In-kind replacement of existing windows 
o Replacement of dilapidated siding 
o New matching paint 
 
The proposed use of Cultural Activity will be contained entirely within the existing Structure, as 
required by the Conservation Easement.  
 
 
 
 
 
114
Analysis and Findings 
Chapter 2 - Zone District Requirements 
The subject property is located partially in the Rural Residential (RR) and partially in the Hillside 
Stewardship Zoning Districts. However, the existing structure is located on the portion of the 
parcel that falls within the Rural Residential (RR) Zoning District.  
 
CODE REQUIREMENT ANALYSIS FINDING 
RR Zone District Intent 
The purpose of the rural residential 
(RR) zone district is to allow existing 
residential uses to remain, allow the 
construction of new single-family 
dwelling units to be constructed upon 
legally platted lots, to allow expansion 
of such uses in accordance with 
limitations and regulations defined in 
the low impact permit process, and 
permit residential uses to be 
developed in accordance with the 
specific provisions of such previously 
approved agreements.  
The subject Structure has 
existed on this site since the 
1920s, long before the Rural 
Residential Zoning District was 
put in place.

Determination of Historic Significance 
According to Development Code 10-8-11(C), “prior to any review for an adaptive reuse of the 
property, the property owner must demonstrate that the structure is ‘historically significant.’” 
Determinations of historic significance shall be made by the Planning Commission. To make a 
115
determination of historic significance, the Planning Commission must find that the structure 
meets at least one definition set forth in 10-8-11(D).

Conditional Use Permit Standards  
No conditional use permit shall be approved unless the applicant demonstrates that: 
  
Standard 1: The use is in accordance with the General Plan. 
  
Analysis: Chapter 5, Objective A states that the County should “Identify and recognize 
amenities important to the community heritage of the Basin and work to preserve such 
amenities to the greatest extent possible.  The County played an important role in the 
settlement of Utah and the West as a whole, with prominent westward trails, early 
116
settlements, and agricultural operations forming the foundation of the Basin. Therefore, 
heritage amenities are defined as:   
a. Sites where culturally significant historic events occurred  
b. Sites important to culturally significant people in history  
c. Historic trails, paths, and other transportation connections and corridors   
d. Structures more than 50 years old  
e. Past and present agricultural operations” 
The determination of Historic Significance will be in alignment with Objective A about 
and the adaptive reuse of the Hi Ute Barn will help to preserve the Structure. 
 
Standard 2: The use conforms to all applicable provisions of this Title, including, but not limited 
to, any applicable provisions of this Section and Chapter 4 of this Title, the General Plan, and 
State and Federal regulations.  
 
Analysis:

Recommendation 
• Staff recommends that the Planning Commission make the determination that the Hi 
Ute Barn is historically significant, according to the findings of fact, conclusions of law, 
and conditions of approval found below.  
• Staff also recommends that the Planning Commission review the proposed Conditional 
Use Permit for Adaptive Reuse of a Historically Significant Structure, open a public 
hearing, and approve the Conditional Use Permit according to the following findings of 
fact, conclusions of law and conditions of approval:  
 
Findings of Fact: 
1. Parcel PP-52-1-A, containing approximately 344.78 acres, is located partially within the 
Rural Residential (RR) Zone and partially within the Hillside Stewardship (HS) Zone. 
2. Parcel PP-52-1-A is owned by Utah Open Lands Conservation Association. 
3. The Applicant, Joseph Geroux, is requesting, on behalf of the property owner, that the 
Planning Commission make a determination of historic significance for the existing 
structure on Parcel PP-52-1-A, also known as the Hi Ute Barn. 
4. The Applicant is also requesting, on behalf of the property owner, a Conditional Use 
Permit for the adaptive reuse of the Historic Structure for Cultural Activities. 
5. The Applicant proposes to make interior structural upgrades for life safety and 
preservation of the barn as well as some exterior restoration with in-kind replacement 
of existing windows, dilapidated siding, and new matching paint.  
122
6. The proposed adaptive reuse falls under the definition of “Cultural Activity” (see below). 
a. CULTURAL ACTIVITY: A facility providing nature exhibitions, libraries, museums, 
art galleries, botanical gardens, historic sites or similar uses displaying, 
preserving, and exhibiting objects, of community and cultural interest in one or 
more of the arts and sciences. 
7. Per Section 10-8-11 of the Snyderville Basin Development Code these uses can be 
approved through a Conditional Use permit, provided the structure is determined to 
have historic significance and the project includes rehabilitating the structure. 
8. The Hi Ute Barn was constructed in 1926, but the Hi Ute Ranch was homesteaded in 
1876.  
9. The barn serves an iconic structure associated with agricultural, farming, and ranching in 
Summit County’s history.  
10. A collaboration of several conservation groups resulted in a Conservation Easement 
(Exhibit D) being placed on the parcel.  
a. This Conservation Easement went through several iterations, each of which are 
attached as Exhibits D-G.  
b. The Conservation Easement anticipated a Use similar to the currently proposed 
Use. 
11. The crux of this proposal is the preservation and rehabilitation of the historic Structure. 
a. Because the applicant is proposing to preserve it, it is eligible for uses on the 
property that otherwise may not be available were it not Historic.  
b. Section 10-8-11 of the Snyderville Basin Development Code states: “The purpose 
of these regulations is to provide for the adaptive reuse of a historically 
significant building with a new use that meets the criteria set forth in this code.” 
12. The Structure was reviewed as part of the 2002 Reconnaissance Survey of Historic 
Buildings and Structures (Exhibit C). The Structure was found to be eligible/significant, 
though it is not currently listed on the National Register of Historic Places. 
13. The Applicant’s Written Description of the Proposal (Exhibit A), states that the following 
modifications are proposed: 
a. Interior structural upgrades for life, safety, and preservation of the barn (with 
respect to seismic events) 
b. Exterior changes are limited to restoring the Structure with: 
i. In-kind replacement of existing windows 
ii. Replacement of dilapidated siding 
iii. New matching paint 
14. The proposed use of Cultural Activity will be contained entirely within the existing 
Structure, as required by the Conservation Easement.  
15. Parking for the use will be contained on site. 
16. All necessary public utilities are available on site 
 
Conclusions of Law: 
1. The Use is in accordance with the General Plan. 
123
2. The Use conforms to all applicable provisions of this Title, including, but not limited to, 
any applicable provisions of this Section and Chapter 4 of this Title, the General Plan, 
and state and federal regulations. 
3. The Use is not detrimental to public health, safety and welfare. 
4. The Use is appropriately located with respect to public facilities. 
5. The Use is compatible with the existing neighborhood character and with the character 
and purpose provision of the applicable zoning district, and will not adversely affect 
surrounding land uses. 
 
Conditions of Approval: 
1. The property owner shall record a deed or restrictive use covenant, to benefit the 
County, which protects the historical Structure from demolition and changes contrary to 
the intent of the preservation of historical structures provision herein. Prior to 
recordation, the deed or restrictive use covenant shall be reviewed and approved by the 
County Attorney’s Office. 
2. The improvements shall be as per the drawings found in Exhibit B of this letter. 
3. The Applicant shall offer shuttle/bus service to reduce any traffic/parking impacts. 
4. All aspects of the Adaptive Reuse shall be contained within the Structure. 
5. ADA access will be reviewed through the building permit process.  
6. No modification to the approved Conditional Use Permit is permitted without the prior 
approval of the Summit County Community Development Department. 
7. All exterior lighting shall comply with Development Code 10-4-22. 
8. All replacement materials and windows shall match existing.  
9. All exterior materials used to replace windows and siding shall be in-kind replacement.  
10. All efforts shall be made to preserve and maintain existing historic materials. 
11. All necessary building permits must be obtained and fees shall be paid prior to the 
commencement of any construction activity, including but not limited to the Summit 
County Engineering and the Summit County Building Departments.  
12. Removal of and disturbance of existing vegetation shall be minimized and revegetated. 
Native vegetation shall be used to reduce the impact of development on steep slopes. 
13. All other service provider requirements shall be met prior to commencement of any 
construction activity.

RE:   Summit County CUP Application  
Hi Ute Barn – Historically Significant Designation & Adaptive Re-Use  
Project Address: 
 
This CUP application is to request the following considerations related to the existing Hi Ute Barn:   
 
• Per Summit County Zoning Ordinance 10-8-11 “Preservation of Historically Significant Structures”  
a.  No Increased Density.  
b.  Use will be contained within the existing structure.  
c.  Determination of Historic Significance: Request Planning Commission designate the 
Barn as “Historically Significant”.  
d.  Defined reasons / justification for designation:  
i.  Hi Ute Barn serves as an iconic structure associated with the events (Agricultural 
/ Farming / Ranching) and have significant contributions to the broad pattern of 
Summit County’s History.  
ii.  The barn structure embodies a distinctive character of open hand built timber 
frame construction built in 1926 and possesses high artistic value along with 
being considered a distinguishable entity.  
iii.  The building has yielded and will yield important historical information.  
e.  Allowed use will be “Cultural Activity”/ Business.  
f.  Qualifying provisions: Due to the unique nature of this project, the immediate need for 
structural upgrades to protect the existing structure from potential seismic events and 
the existing conservation easement protections regarding any material change to the 
exterior we are requesting concurrent processing to designate the barn structure as 
“Historically Significant” along with the conditional use permit allowing the adaptive 
re-use. The adaptive re-use is primarily for interior structural upgrades for life safety and 
preservation of the existing barn with respect to potential seismic events. Any exterior 
changes will be limited to restoring the barn to its original character with in kind 
replacement of existing windows, replacement of dilapidated siding and new matching 
paint. The adaptive re-use will be contained entirely within the historically significant 
structure as required by the existing conservation easement. Distinctive features, 
finishes and construction techniques will be preserved.

CUP Descriptions 
• Hours of operation; Seasonal (April – October) Limited to standard business hours 
• Number of Employees; No on site employees.  
• Traffic; Minimal traffic during business hours. Planned shuttle/bus service to reduce impact. 
Estimated 5-8 trips per month during April - October. 
• Parking; No changes. Shuttle or single bus use existing gravel parking area.  
• Signs; No new signage. Existing sign is per conservation easement.  
• Lighting; No exterior changes. Limited to interior only.   
• Removal of Landscape; limited to wildland fire mitigation per PC Fire Department. No other 
changes.  
• Noise, Fumes, Odors; No applicable.  
 
 
Background 
 
The Hi Ute ranch was originally homesteaded in 1876, and the famous barn was constructed in 1926. The 
Hi Ute Barn served as the main agricultural building located at the Hi Ute Ranch in Summit County, Utah. 
With the transformation of the Snyderville Basin in the past 100 years from a rural and agricultural area 
to a more populated lifestyle based living and resort center, the Hi Ute Barn and Hi Ute Ranch open 
space remain as an Iconic legacy to the past. The surrounding and protected open space is more critical 
today with the added pressure of development and density encroachment from all sides. Given the 
proximity and heavy vehicular traffic along Interstate 80 the ranch and more specifically, the “Barn” has 
special cultural and historical significance. The Hi Ute Ranch pastures were preserved in 2003 through a 
significant funding effort led by Utah Open Lands and some funds from Summit County’s Snyderville 
Basin Special Recreation District and a donation from the landowner of a portion of the conservation 
easement.  The Conservation Easement includes the protection of the iconic barn structure as well as the 
protection of the properties scenic value under I.R.C. 170(h)(4)(A)(iii).  Public recreational, continued 
agricultural and educational and cultural uses are all permitted under the conservation easement as well 
as with the donation of the fee title to Utah Open Lands the ability to use the property for the 
organizations conservation mission. 
 
Adaptive Re-Use Design Strategy 
 
The scenic and conservation significance of this project provides the rational for a sustainable Adaptive 
Re-Use approach to the existing Barn structure. The adaptive re-use seeks to reach a Net Zero or better 
Carbon Negative project addressing the needed structural upgrades and in-kind replacement of existing 
dilapidated elements. The environmental benefits by reusing the existing Barn minimize the need for 
newly supplied materials. The projects carbon footprint is further reduced by using locally and regionally 
supplied materials to the greatest extent possible. The life safety upgrades include new interior 
reinforcement of the structural components to address lateral forces. The exterior elements are limited 
to in kind replacement of the existing windows, repairing the existing wood siding and newly matched 
exterior paint.

Land Type
The open landdescribedinthisbaselinedocumentation istheHi-Ute Ranch (also
referredtoas"theProperty"inthisbaselinedocument; Figure2).The Property
comprisesapproximately178 acres,justwest ofInterstateHighway 80,about7 miles
northwestofPark CityinSummit County,Utah. The Propertyconsistsofportionsof
Sections13 and 24 inTownship 1 South,Range 3 East,SaltLake base and meridian.
ExactparceldescriptionsarerecordedintheDeed ofConservationEasement.
The Propertyconsistsofabeautiful,montane landscapewith quaking aspen (Populus
tremuloides)and mountain shrubsaround grasspastureland(Figure3).The Propertyis
contiguouswiththeremarkable,undeveloped open landof3-Mile Canyon. Three-Mile
Creek runsfrom thecanyon throughtheProperty.Severalponds have been createdalong
thelengthof3-Mile Creek and intwo otherplaceson theProperty;some oftheseponds
canbe seenfrom Highway I-80.Next tothebottom ponds on 3-Mile Creek standsthe
whiteHi-Ute barn,which isa landmark inthearea.Two homes, atack-shed,ahorse
barn,and aridingcorralarefound closetotheHi-Ute barn. Tallnarrowleafcottonwoods
(Populusangustifolia)grow nearthesouthwesternedge oftheProperty.
Summit County has been growing rapidlyinrecentyears,supportingtheneed topreserve
naturalhabitatfrom thepressuresofrapidhousing expansion inthearea.Open landlike
thatofthePropertyhas alreadybeen permanently replacedby subdivisionsand other
buildingsand infrastructureadjacenttothePropertyon theeast,a schoolhasbeen built
adjacenttothenorthwest(EckerMiddle School),and subdivisionsareexpanding inthe
mountains tothewest oftheProperty.
The landowner retainstitletotheProperty,butwillestablisha ConservationEasement
thatwillprotecttheopen-space (conservation)valuesoftheProperty.The Conservation
Easement willpreservethescenicvalueoftheProperty,a significantpublicresource.
Mountains, vegetation,and thehistoricbarn on thePropertyform a significantfeatureof
thesceneryviewed from Interstate80,Kilby Road (thefrontageroad),and a
bicycle/pedestrianpathparallelingKilby Road. The Propertyalsopreservesvaluable
ecologicalcommunities, currentlyincludingmountain riparian,standingwater,flowing
water,aspen woodland, and sagebrush/grasslandwildlifehabitat,which arerecognizedas
highpriorityhabitatsforconservationinUtah (Gorrelletal.2005, Parrishetal.2002).
The Propertyalsopreservesrelativelynaturalconditionsalong3-Mile Creek (Figure4),
which helpstopreservehigh-qualitystreamwaters. Incontrast,development of
buildingsorroadson thePropertywould detractfrom thescenicand ecological
conservationvaluesoftheProperty.

Conservation Values
PreservationoftheHi-Ute Propertywillpreservethefollowingvaluesoftheland:
EcologicalSystem: Montane
The PropertyoccursintheWasatch Mountain range oftheMiddle Rocky Mountains.
The Propertyexperiencesrelativelyshortsummers and coldwinterswith abundant snow.
Elevationson thePropertyrangefrom approximately6,300 ftalongthenortheasternedge
ofthePropertytoapproximately6,500 fton thehillsidesalongthesouthwesternsideof
theProperty.The propertyhasbeen influencedby ranching,horsepasture,and
landscapingactivities;however, most ofthePropertyisinarelativelynaturalcondition.
Currentlyhabitaton thePropertyincludesavarietyofplantsand wildlife(e.g.,Figure5).
The Propertyincludesapproximately3000 feetofThreemile Canyon Creek,nine(9)
smallponds,a sagebrush(Artemisiatridentata)-coveredknolland hillsides,and patches
ofaspen,narrowleafcottonwood, and mountain shrubs(e.g.,serviceberry[Amelanchier
utahensis],chokecherry[Prunus virginiana],and snowberry [Symphoricarpos
oreophilus])adjacenttograsspasture.Flowing water,standingwater,mountain riparian,
aspen,and sagebrushsteppehabitats,such asthosefound on theProperty,have been
listedashighpriorityhabitatsforconservationinUtah by theUtah DivisionofWildlife
Resources (Gorrelletal.2005) and orUtah PartnersinFlight(Parrishetal.2002).
Wildlifeand plantson thePropertyincludenativespeciesadaptedtothemountains such
asBonnevillecutthroattrout(Oncorhynchus clarkiutah).Because thecurrentlandowner
ofthePropertydoes notallowfishingon theProperty,and because thewaterways on the
Propertyarerichwith amphipods and otheraquaticlarvae,and because 3-Mile Canyon
Creek has a few sectionsofgravelthoughttobe appropriateforspawning, theProperty
probablyforms avaluablerefugeforBonnevillecutthroattrout.A cutthroattroutthought
tobe aBonnevillecutthroattrout(> 14 inches)was observedinone oftheponds on the
Property.Bonnevillecutthroattroutarenativetotheareaand have been documented in
theUS Geologic Survey quadranglemap (quad)thatcontainstheProperty(ParkCity
West quad) and adjacentquads. Bonnevillecutthroattroutarelistedasakey speciesfor
conservationinUtah (TierI;Gorrelletal.2005).
Although no threatenedorendangered specieswere observed on theProperty,itprovides
habitatappropriateforseveralpriorityspeciesforconservationinUtah inadditionto
Bonnevillecutthroattrout,includingferruginoushawks (Buteoregalis),bobolinks
(Dolichonyxoryzivorus),westerntoads(Bufo boreas),lewis'swoodpeckers (Melanerpes
lewis),and Columbia spottedfrogs(Rana luteiventris);allofthesespecieshave been
documented intheareaoftheProperty(ParkCityWest quad) since1988 (Appendix 2).
The relativelylargespatialextentoftheProperty(particularlyinconjunctionwith
Threemile Canyon, seebelow) allowsthePropertytosupportmany oftheresourcesand
spaceofvaluetoplantsand wildlife,includinglargeanimalssuch asmule deer(aTierIII
key speciesforconservationinUtah; Gorrelletal.2005),elk(Cervus canadensis),
moose (Alcesalces),and predatorssuch asmountain lions(Puma concolor).Wildlife
18
214
detectedorreportedon thepropertyincludemule deer,elk,beaver (Castorcanadensis),
moose, and mountain lions.
The Propertyiscontiguouswithundeveloped 3-MileCanyon tothesouthwest.Three-
Mile Canyon isabeautifulcanyon with dense conifers,mountain shrubs,and the
headwatersofThreemile Canyon Creek. The landowner oftheHi-Ute Propertyowns
1300 acresin3-Mile Canyon adjacenttotheProperty.The Propertyforms a spatial
bufferbetween Highway I-80and Threemile Canyon. Reciprocally,theecologicalvalue
ofthePropertyisenhanced by being contiguouswith 3-Mile Canyon naturalarea.
Figure 5.Red-tailedhawk flyingcloseforalook.(Waypoint 30)
Open Space for Scenic Enjoyment
The Propertyprovidesabeautifulview from InterstateHighway 80,Kilby Road
(frontageroad),and abicycle/pedestrianpaththatrunsnexttoKilby Road alongthe
Property.Ecker PublicMiddle School isalsoadjacenttotheProperty.The open space
ofthePropertyprovidesvisualrelieffrom housing development intheareaofthe
Property.
Cultural
Open spacesuch asthePropertycontributestothemountain culturefavoredinmany
partsofSummit County, and helpspreservesceneryand wildlifethatcontributeto
attractingpeopletothePark Cityareaand theWasatch Mountains. The Property
preserveslandthatcontributestoa senseofopenness and wildness.
19
215
Other open space values
Watershed Protection:PreservationofthePropertywillhelptopreventdegradationof
Threemile Canyon Creek thatcouldresultfrom development. Threemile Canyon Creek
runsdirectlyintoEastCanyon Creek. Threemile Canyon Creek supportsfishand
provideswater forirrigationand recreationdownstream. Therefore,degradationof
Threemile Canyon Creek would contributetothediminutionofpublicvalues(fishery,
irrigation,and recreation)offoftheProperty.
Agriculture:Soilofmost ofthePropertyisconsideredprime farmland soilifirrigated
(soiltype:Manila-Ant Flatloams,2 to8 percentslopes;areaon Property:~130 acres
[~70% ofPropertyarea];NRCS 2007).
Ownership
The Propertyisowned by thePaulBeuhner. There areno inholdings.Mineralrightson
thePropertyareowned by Union PacificRailroad.
Easements
InadditiontotheConservationEasement forwhich thisbaselinedocument serves,other
easements existon theProperty(e.g.,utilities;on filewithUtah Open Lands). A
bicycle/pedestrianpathway occursinan easement on thePropertyalongKilby Road
(Figure6a).

Current Land Use
Currentlanduseby humans consistsofdwellingintheexistingtwo houses (Figures2d
and 6c);normal use oftheHi-Ute Ranch barn,otheroutbuildings,and ridingcorral;use
ofthefencednortheastpasturefor3 to5 horses;and use ofexistingprivateroadsfor
accesstothePropertyand 3-Mile Canyon. Itwas seenthatvegetationand debrisina
pond outflowwas clearedby hand tomaintainstreamflow. Evidence was observed of
wheeled,motorized vehicleuse on dirtroadson theProperty,and inotherareasnot on
themain dirtroads.Itappearsfrom thelimitednatureand locationofvehicletracks,that
off-roadvehicleuse occursfori-anchingand propertymaintenance ratherthanrecreation.
Much ofthePropertyisgrasspasturethatisgrazedby horsesduringthegrowing season,
and issometimes mowed forhay. Wire fenceshave been constructedon thePropertyfor
livestockrestrictionand orpropertydelineation.Wooden-rail fenceshave been
constructedinsteadofwire fencesinhighlyvisibleareas.Pastureareasareflood
irrigated.Severalditchesforwater-conveyance occuron theProperty.Landscaping of
thebuildingsby theHi-Ute Ranch Barn appeartobe sprinklerirrigatedwith wellwater.
The landowner currentlyprohibitsfishing,hunting,trapping,and publictrespasson the
Property.

History
The areaoftheHi-Ute Ranch hasbeen used forranchingsincearound theturnofthe20th
century.Fences on thenorthwestpartofthePropertyremain from elkranching,which
occurredforaboutayearinthelate1990's.No buildingsbesidesthosecurrentlyon the
Propertyareknown tohave been constructedon theProperty.No mining isknown to
have occurredon theProperty.

landon allothersidesofthePropertyisprivate.

Propertyfrom thewest (Figure10b). Pinebrook Open Space isadjacenttothenorthof

Space isnotcurrentlyprotectedby a conservationeasement. Ecker Middle School has
been builttothenorthadjacenttotheProperty(Figure2a).Three-Mile Canyon occurs
adjacenttothePropertytothesouthwest(figureslb,lc,and 2b). Currentlythe
landowner ofthePropertyowns 1300 acresinThreemile Canyon. The onlybuildingsin
Threemile Canyon aretwo houses/cabinsbelongingtotheLandowner oftheProperty.

Hazardous and Toxic Materials
No known hazardous ortoxicmaterialsareknown on thePropertyorwere reportedby
thelandowner tobe locatedon theProperty nor were signsdetectedthatindicated
hazardous ortoxicmaterialreleases(e.g.,dead animals,fluiddischarge,inexplicablesoil
disturbance,soildiscoloration,etc.).Common hazardous and toxicmaterialssuch as
paint,fuel,and household cleaningsuppliesarethoughttobe storedindoorsatthe
developed areaneartheHi-Ute Ranch Barn,butno dumping orleakingofthistypeof
materialwas detectedduringbaselineobservations.A thorough investigationof
buildingsand storedmaterialswas notaccomplished duringbaselineobservations.
Parkingand operationofmotor vehiclesoccurson theProperty,but thisactivitydoes not
appeartohave resultedinsignificantreleasesofoilorothermaterialon theProperty.
Utah GIS data(LUST and Derr Master Index)do notindicateany sitesofhazardous
materialon theProperty,nor any leakingunderground storagetanks.1

Threats and Management Suggestions
Residentialor commercial development oftheProperty
Development ofbuildingsand infrastructureon thePropertywould directlydiminishthe
wildlifehabitatand scenicvaluesoftheProperty,and would arguablynegativelyaffect
theculturalvalueofthePropertyaswellasprobably degrading3-Mile Canyon Creek.
OutstandingconservationvaluesofthePropertywarrantperpetualpreservationofthe
Propertyfrom any riskofresidentialorcommercial development.
Residentialorcommercial development oftheadjacentland
Development ofadjacentlandposes athreattotheconservationvaluesoftheProperty
because development impacts surroundinglandinmany ways, including:introducing
disturbances(e.g.,noise,lights,and pets),changinghydrologicprocesses(throughwater
use and constructionofimperviouslandcover),increasingtheriskofhazardous materials
enteringtheeasement Propertyand surfacewater on theProperty(duringconstruction
and chronicallyaslandowners bringand use hazardous materialson theirpropertyand as
runofffrom roadswashes materialfrom pollutedsurfacesintothelandand surface
water),and focusingwildlifeuse on a smallerarea.Itwould be welliflandowners
permanently protectedopen landadjacenttothePropertyfrom commercial or
residentialdevelopment inordertopreservethesame kindsofconservationvaluesas
thosethatexiston theProperty.
Roads on oradjacenttotheProperty
Roads have directand indirectnegativeeffectson ecosystems. Road constructionand
maintenance involvesdisruptionofnativesoil,plant,and animal communities and is
oftenassociatedwith erosionofsoiland infestationofinvasiveweeds alongroad
margins. The presenceofroadsand motorized vehicleuse can presentchroniceffects
such aserosion,noise,contaminatedrunoff,and soildisturbance.Soilsbecome
compacted on roads.No new roads should be constructedon theEasement Property.
Motorized vehicleuseshould be restrictedtoexistingroads on theProperty exceptfor
emergencies ornecessarymaintenance oftheProperty. Ifany road on theProperty is
or becomes unnecessary,itwould be welltocloseitand restoredesirablevegetationon
it.
Non-native,invasiveplants
Invasiveplantspose athreattoexistingecosystem structureand functions.Currently
invasivespeciessuch asCanada thistle(Cirsiumarvense)arefound on theProperty.
Canada thistleisparticularlyabundant intheriparianareas.Controlofinvasiveplantson
theEasement Propertymay itselfthreatennativeplantsand associatedmicro and macro
organisms ifchemical controlmethods areused. Careful,consistenteffortstoreduce and
controlthespread ofinvasiveplantson theProperty areadvised. These methods may
includephysicalremoval ofinvasivespecies,usingchemicalherbicides,plantingseeds
orplantstoreplaceand compete withinvasivespecieswhere appropriate,and
preventingtransportofcontaminated materialsonto theProperty.
38
234
Controlofnuisance wildhfe
Wildlifecontrolmay threatenothernon-targetwildlifeand orwildlifeon thePropertyif
poisonsorindiscriminantlethaltrappingmethods areemployed on adjacentlandsoron
theProperty,includingrodentpoisonsand predatorcontrol.Itwould be welltoeducate
adjacenthomeowners about environmentallyappropriatediscouragement and controlof
nuisancewildhfe.Landowner supportfor appropriatewildhfecontrolmethods willbe
needed.
Farming/Ranching
While farmingand ranchingpreservehistoricallyimportantand currentlyvaluable
lifestylesand resources,farming and ranchingpracticescan degradenaturalvaluesofa
landscape.Itwould be welltotakecaretopracticesustainable,conservation-oriented
agriculturalpracticeson theProperty. The landowner may wish todevelopwritten
ranchingand orfarming plans withconsultationofagriculturaland conservation
professionals.A formalized,writtenplan can helpguide ranch managers. Care must be
takentoassessand adapt agriculturalpracticestochanging conditionson and adjacent
tothePropertyas theyinfluencetheConservation Values.
Climate change
Climate change may causechanges tovegetationand animalson theHi-Ute Property.
Currentlythestructureand functionofthehabitatismaintainedby coldwinterswith
abundant snow and warm summers. Changes totheplantand animal communities ofthe
Easement Propertyresultingfrom a foreseeabletrendofwarmer temperaturesare
unpredictable.Indirecteffectsofchronicwarming may be significant,and may include
more severedisturbancessuch increasedpredationfrom insectherbivores,prolonged
drought,and increasedinvasionby non-nativeorganisms. Management plansfor the
Propertywillneed tobe adaptableand adapted tochanging climateand otherlarge-
scaleenvironmentalchanges.
Stewardship and Management
Stewardshipofthelandisthedirectresponsibilityofthelandowner,who willactively
manage activitieson theProperty.The ConservationEasement holder,Utah Open
Lands,willhelpwithmanagement strategiestopreservetheconservationvalues.The
landowner and Utah Open Lands willhave theadded responsibilityformonitoringthe
Propertyand enforcingtheConservationEasement.

ECOLOGY
Ecoregion
The Hi-Ute PropertyispartoftheWasatch and Uintah Mountains ecoregionand has
been classifiedspecificallyintheMountain Valleys(Woods etal.2001). The Property
occursinwhat Stokes(1986)classifiedastheWasatch Mountains physiographicsection
on theborderoftheWasatch HinterlandsoftheMiddle Rocky Mountains physiographic
provmce.
Climate
Temperatures intheareaofthePropertyrange from below zerodegreesFahrenheit(F)
duringthewintermonths ofNovember throughMarch toover90 degreesF duringthe
summer months ofJulyand August (personalobservation),with amean average
temperatureofabout40 to45 degreesF (NRCS 2007),and an averageannualminimum
temperatureof-20to-10 degreesF (HardinessZone 5).
2 The annualfrost-freeperiod
lasts60 to90 days (NRCS 2007),with an estimated30 to60 days above 86 deg.F (Heat
Zone 5 or6). Annual precipitationisabout 16 to22 inches(Woods etal.2001, NRCS
2007)
Broad climatedescriptionsforthePropertygeneralizesmall-scaleenvironmental
differencesthatcorrespondwithvery specificconditions.Small-scaleenvironmental
variabilityoccurson thePropertydue toabioticvariables(elevation,slopeaspect,water,
slopesteepness,ground material,positionrelativetootherphysicalfeatures,etc.),and to
bioticvariables(plantspecies,plantheight,plantdensity,animaluse,etc.).Some small-
scaleenvironmentalvariabilitycan be generallyrecognized.For example, asinother
montane systems,slopesfacingsouthand west aredrierand hotterthanslopesfacing
northand east;riparianareasarecoolerand wetterthanhillsides;denselyvegetatedareas
providethermalshelter;and soforth.These and othersmall-scaleclimatevariations
contributetodesirablebiologicaldiversity.

Water
Surface water
Permanent (perennial)surfacewater on thePropertyisThreemile Creek and ponds.
Other surfacewateroccursinirrigationditcheson theProperty.
Threemile Creek isa first-orderstreamwith originsinThreemile Canyon. Water isclear.
Substrateisgraveland cobblewith abundant algae(speciesunknown) and sediment in
places.Stream flow and geomorphology have been alteredby landscapingincluding
pond constructionon thePropertyand upstream. Channel dimensions were identifiedat
8 locations(bywaypoint 8,10,15,and 19;between waypoints 11 and 14,18 and 22;two
between waypoints 15 and 16).Dimentions representonlytheactive(wet)channeland
theentireincisedchannelbecause itwas notpossibletoaccuratelyidentifybankfull
channeldue tosnow cover(Figure12).At thetime ofbaselineobservation,wet width
was 3 ft(standarddeviation[SD] = 0.5ft)and wet depthwas 0.5ft(SD = 0 ft).The
entirechannel(from valleybottom tobottom ofchannel)averaged 15 ft(SD = 4 ft)wide
and 5 ft(SD = 1ft)deep,foramean width todepthratioof3 (SD = 1).Stream length
was measured on a2006 high resolutionorthophotograph(HRO; 1 ftresolution;
12TVL520080) usingArcMap (v.9.2,ESRI), forstreamsegments between ponds (5
segments). TotallengthofThreemile Creek on thePropertywas 3,010 ft.Sinuositywas
computed asthechannel (curved)lengthdividedby thestraightdistancebetween the
pointswhere thestreamexitedtheupstream pond and enteredthedownstream pond.
Sinuositywas 1.17(SD = 0.6).

Vegetation
Plant communities
Four major plantcommunities,typifiedby locationoreasilyvisibleplantspeciesoccur
on theProperty:riparian,sagebrush/grass,quaking aspen (Populus tremuloides),and
grass.Ripariancommunities occur alongThreemile Creek and ponds (Table 1,Figure
13);theyaremarked by patchywillows,butmuch ofthecreekbank vegetationisgrass.
Standsofnarrowleafcottonwoods (Populusangustifolia)occurinthenortheasterncorner
ofthePropertyalongdiversionditchesand Pond 8. Sagebrush/grasscommunities
(Figure14)includebigsagebrush(Artemisiatridentata),grasses,and insome places
othershrubssuch asrubberrabbitbrush(Ericamerianauseosa) and serviceberry
(Amelanchierutahensis).Quaking aspen communities occurprimarilyalongtheknollin
thesouthwesternpartofthePropertyand nearthesouthwestern-mostcornerofthe
Property.Quaking aspen communities asdelineatedon baselinemaps includemountain
shrubssuch asserviceberryand choke cherry(Prunus virginiana),and Gambel oak
(Quercus gambelii)insome places.Standsofspruce(mostthoughttobe Piceapungens)
and a few lodgepolepines(Pinuscontorta)occurinplaceson theProperty,and appearto
have been plantedby humans. The largestconiferstandon thePropertyoccursnearthe
northeastcorneroftheProperty.Grass communities existaspastureson theProperty;
theyincludesmooth brome (Bromus inermis),crestedwheatgrass(Agropyron cristatum),
orchardgrass(Dactylisglomerata),timothy (Phleum pratense),and othergrassesand
forbs.

InvasivePlants
Non-native,invasiveplantsarefound throughouttheProperty.Canada thistle(Cirsium
arvense)iscommon inriparianareas.Some non-nativegrassspeciescommon on the
Property(e.g.,smooth brome) may be consideredinvasive.Invasivespecieswere not
thoroughlymapped duringbaselineassessmentdue tothelatenessoftheseasonand the
requiredtime forsuch an extensiveproject;however, landowner controlofinvasive
plantson thePropertycouldreasonablybegin with a focuson roadsidesand riparian
areas.

habitatforconservationinUtah (Gorrelletal.2005). Similarly,Utah Partnersinflight
listsmountain riparianhabitatasaveryhighpriorityforconservationforbirdsinUtah
(Parrishetal.2002). Sagebrush/grasslandhabitatisconsideredahighpriorityhabitatfor
conservationforwildlifeingeneraland forbirdsinUtah (Parrishetal.2002, Gorrellet
al.2005). Flowing water,standingwater,and aspen habitatsarealsoconsideredamong
the10top-priorityhabitatsforconservationforwildlifeinUtah (Gorrelletal.2005).

Wildlife
The largesizeoftheHi-Ute Ranch particularlyinconjunctionwiththeadjacent
Threemile Canyon helpstoensurethepresenceand viabilityofmany speciesofwildlife,
includingthosethatrequirerelativelyextensivelandscapes,such asmountain lions
(Puma concolor),and raptors.Severalanimal specieswere observed,ortheirtracks,
scat,orburrows were observedduringbaselineobservations;however, baseline
observationsrepresentonlya fractionoftheactualspeciespresent.Additionalspecies
may be presentatsome time(s)(e.g.,duringthewarm months, breedingseason,or
migration).
Mammals
The Propertyprovidesfood,water,and shelterresourcesformammals; mammals arein
turnintegraltotheecosystem. Small mammal herbivoresand thosethatfeedon seeds
and fruitsinfluencevegetationand disperseseeds.Some kindsofsmallmammals also
feedon invertebratessuch ascricketsand grasshoppers.The burrows ofsmallmammals
provideshelterforotheranimalsand arethoughttoaidinsoilaerationand water
infiltration.Badger (Taxideataxus)holeswere observed on theProperty,aswere the
burrows ofother,smallermammals. Small mammals form abase ofprey speciesfor
predatorybirds,reptiles,and mammals. Weasel (Mustelaspp.)and coyote(Canis
latrans)trackswere observedon theProperty.Evidence ofcurrentlyactivebeavers
(Castorcandensis)was observedduringbaselinedocumentation visits.Beavers are
nativetotheareaand linkstreamsand riparianareas.Largermammals on theProperty
influencesoilstructurewhere theytravel,and theirherbivorymay influencevegetation
abundance and species.Large herbivoresprovideprey forlargepredators,and are
commonly culturallydesirableasgame animalsand foraestheticreasons.Mule deer
(Odocoileushemionus),and elk(Cervus canadensis)tracksand trailswere observed on
theProperty.Moose (Alcesalces)have been seenatothertimesneartheProperty,and
moose browsing isevidentinriparianareasoftheWeber River on theProperty.The
landowner reportsthatmountain lions(Puma concolor)have been seenon ornearthe
Property.Large mammals requirelargeareasofappropriatehabitat;theProperty
providesa substantialamount ofhabitatforlargemammals, particularlyinassociation
with adjacentopen lands.
Mammals listedasspecialconcern forconservation:UDWR listsseveralmammal
speciesofconservationneed inUtah thatmay occurnow orinthefutureon theProperty
(Gorrelletal.2005);ofthespecieson thislist,onlymule deerareknown tocurrently
occuron theProperty.No threatened,endangered,orsensitive(TES) mammals were
detectedon thePropertyduringbaselineobservations,and none were reportedon the
Property.Townsend's big-earedbats(Corynorhinus townsendiiBf specialconcernfor
conservationinUtah-have been documented in1998 inUSGS quads adjacenttothe
Property(Brightonquad and Dromedary Peak quads;Appendix 2).

vernalis)-a reptileofspecialconcern forconservationinUtah thathasbeen documented

Invertebrates

obligationorresponsibilityforpayment oftaxesorassessmentsleviedupon any oftheProperty.
ASSIGNMENT
10.Transfer ofEasement. IfGRANTEE determinesthatitno longerisabletoperform its
obligationsorenforceitsrightsunder thisAgreement, orthatitno longerdesirestoenforcesaid
rights,orifGRANTEE ceasestoexist,orisotherwisepreventedfrom enforcingitsrightsunder
thisAgreement, orifGRANTEE no longerqualifiesasa "qualifiedorganization"under Section
170 (h)(3)oftheCode ora comparable provisioninany subsequentrevisionoftheIRS Code,
GRANTEE shallassoon aspracticalconvey inperpetuityallitsrightsunder thisinstrumentand
delivera copy ofthisinstrumenttoan organizationdesignatedby GRANTEE and describedin,
orcontemplatedby,Section170(h)(3)oftheCode orthecomparable provisioninany
subsequentrevisionoftheCode, withoutqualification,toensurethatthisEasement isenforced.
GRANTEE ishereby expresslyprohibitedfrom subsequentlytransferringthisEasement,
whether ornotforconsideration,unless:

purposeswhich thisEasement isintendedtoadvance continuetobe carriedout;

under Section170(h)(3)oftheCode (orthecomparable provisioninany subsequent

GRANTOR isnotprohibitedfrom transferringitsinterestinthePropertyprovidedthatany

AMENDMENT: EXTINGUISHMENT
11.Limitations on Amendments. Ifcircumstancesariseunder which an amendment toor
modificationofthisEasement would be appropriate,GRANTOR and GRANTEE may, by
mutual writtenagreement,jointlyamend thisEasement; provided thatno amendment shallbe
made thatwilladverselyaffectthequalificationofthisEasement ofthestatusofGRANTEE
under any applicablelaws,includingSections170(h)and 501(c)(3)oftheCode and thelaws of
theStateofUtah. Any such amendment shallbe consistentwith thepurpose ofthisEasement,
shallnotaffectitsperpetualduration,and shallnotpermitany materialimpairment ofthe
ConservationValues. Any such amendment shallbe filedintheCounty Recorder'sOfficeof
Summit County, Utah. Nothing inthisparagraph shallrequireGRANTOR orGRANTEE to
agreetoany amendment ortoconsultornegotiateregardingany amendment.
12,Limitations on Extinguishment. Ifcircumstancesariseinthefuturethatrenderthe
purpose ofthisEasement impossibletoaccomplish,thisEasement can onlybe terminatedor
extinguished,whether with respecttoallorpartoftheProperty,by judicialproceedingsina
courtofcompetent jurisdiction.Insuch event,allproceeds receivedby theGRANTEE from a
subsequentsaleorexchange ofthePropertymust be used by GRANTEE ina manner consistent
withtheConservationValues asrequiredby TreasuryRegulationSection1.170A-14(g)(6)(i)or
any subsequentrevisiontothatsection.
12.1Property Right. As requiredby Treasury RegulationSection1.170A-14(g)(6)(ii),
GRANTOR agreesthatthisEasement has given GRANTEE a fullyvestedpropertyrightinthe
Propertywith a fairmarket valuethatisatleastequaltotheproportionatevaluethatthis
Easement bearstothevalueofthePropertyasa whole, each asofthedatehereof.When a
change inconditionsgiverisetotheextinguishmentofthisEasement asprovided forabove,
GRANTEE shallbe entitledtoreceivea portionoftheresultingproceeds which areatleastequal
tothatproportionatevalueoftheEasement. Allsuch proceedsreceivedby GRANTEE shallbe
used by GRANTEE ina manner consistentwith theConservationValues,

Land Type
The open landdescribedinthisbaselinedocumentation(referredtoas"theProperty")is
approximately1280 acresofThreemileCanyon, Summit County,Utah.The exactparcel
descriptionisrecordedintheDeed ofConservationEasement.
The Propertyconsistsofarelativelynaturalcanyon withstivamsand ponds,and
surroundinguplands.ThreemileCanyon formsasignificantpartoftheviewscapefrom
1-80and nearbyareas.HillsidesofthePropertycanbe seenformiles.From 1-80,the
view encompasses up to2 milesfrom theroadtothefurthestextentsoftheProperty
approximately1,500feethigheron mountain sidesatthehead ofthecanyon. Elevation
ofthePropertyvariesfmm approximately6,500ftto8,000ftelevation.The Property
contributessubstantiallytosceneryofaclasscomparabletothemost beautifulmountain
sceneryintheworldi.The PropertyhelpstodefinetheParkCityareavisuallyand
aesthetically.Vegetationcommunities on thePropertyincludequakingaspen(Populus
tremuloides),conifer(mainlyAbiesconcolor,and Pseudorsuga menzies#),mountain
brush(includingGambel oak {Quercus gambelii),bigtoothmaple (Acer
grandidentatum),and mountain mahogany {Cercocarpusledifolia}),sagebrush
(Artemisiatridentata),and grasses,rushes,and forbs.Plantson thePropertycontributeto
year-roundbeauty:greensinthespringand summer, avarietyofautumn colorsincluding
yellow,red,and orange;and darkconifersagainstwhitesnow inthewinter.
Vegetationcommunities and surfacewateron thePropertyfunctiontoprovideresources
formany speciesofwildlifeincludingmigratorybirdssuchasbroad-tailed
hummingbirds (Selasphorusplatycercus),raptors,largemammals suchasmule deer
(Odocoileushemionus),elk(Cervuscanadensis),moose (Alcesalces),and predatorssuch
asmountain lions(Puma concolor),and blackbears(Ursusamericanus). Plant
communitiesarerelativelydiversebecauseofthepresenceofperennialsurfacewater,
topographicdiversity,and arecenthistoryofconservation-mindedstewardshipby the
landowner.The Propertyprovideshabitatappropriateforseveralspeciesthatare
currentlypriorityforconservationinUtah,including:Bonnevillecutthroattrout
(Oncorhynchusclarkiutah)thathave been documented inthearea;smooth greensnakes
(Opheodrysvernalis)thathave been documented nearbyincontiguousopen space;
northerngoshawks (Accipitergentilis),westerntoads(Bufoboreas),Columbia spotted
frogs(Rana luteiventris),and othersthathave been documented inthearea(Appendix 2).
Plantand animalcommunities provideecosystemsfunctionssuchasprovidinghigh-
qualitywatertotheEastCanyon Creek watershed,and arealsoattractiveforpublic
educationand recreation.
The Propertyprovidesexcellentpotential.foroutdooreducation,bothasamodel of
relativelynaturalsystemsand asan accessibleareaforoutdoorexperiences.The Property
alsoprovidesopportunitiesforactiverecreationon existingroadsand trails,possibly
includehikingand cross-countryskiingunderprimitiveconditions,aswellasother
recreationalactivitiessuchasphotography,painting,birdwatching,and meditation.The

Conservation Values
The Propertylirovidesopeh spaceinaremar ablyscenicareaofUtah.Itprovidesscenic,
natural/ecological,educational,recreatioital,and agriculturalvahles.
Conservaiionv2duesofthePropeHvinclude:
1. Scenic:The Propertycontribiltesremarkablevisualbeautyas.aprominent,
definitivecomponent oftheviewscapenearParkCityfrom InterstateHighway 80
(1-80),from theOlympic Ski1ark.Itisalsovisiblefrontpublichikingtrailsin
neaibymountains,includingthepopularmid-mountain traitThe Propertyisa
substantialpartofworld class:mountainscebery?The canyon itselfisbeautifulin
shapeasitsweeps from neatlythelevelofI-80tohighmountainsand ridges.
Vegetationcommunitieson-thepropertyarescenicyestround Shades ofgreen
figureprominentlyinthespringand summer months;patchesofvividyellows
(quakingaspensand others),reds(bigtooth:mapl6sand.others),and shadesof .
oratige(Gambel oak md others).interspersewithdark-greenconifers,and paler
patchesofsagebrushand grassifthefall;findpalesteinsand-branchesstand.

00936728 Page 55 of98 _Summit county
309
above thewhitesweep ofsnow withpatchesand punctuationsofdarkconifers
duringthewinter.At smallerscales,thePropertyprovidesabundantbeautiful
nooks and vistasforhikersoranyone on thePropertyitself,suchasclearwater
runningthroughlushplants,reflectivebeaverponds,glimpsesofwildlife,etc.
Natural:The propertysupportswetland,riparian,aspen,mountain brush,
sagebrush,and othercommunities.Plantcommunities providevisualbeautyas
wellasresourcesforwildlifeand functionfundamentallyinnutrientcyclingand
energyflowsintheecosystem.The Propertyencompasses about 1500 vertical
feet.oftopographicchange from easttowest,and includessub-sitesand pockets
thatgivevariety.Perennialwateron portionsofthepropertycontributestothe
diversityofvegetationand babitats.Excellentspring,summer, and fallhabitatfor
mule deerand elkand mountain lions.Year-roundhabitatformoose. High value
habitatforblackbear.Two prioritywildlifespeciesdocumented on theProperty:
Bonnevillecutthroattroutand mule deer.At leastfourotherpriorityspecieshave
beendocumented intheareawithapparentlyappropriatehabitaton theProperty:
Columbia spottedfrog,northerngoshawk, smooth greensnake,and westerntoad.
Beaversarepresentinthisvalleywhere theiractivitiescanoccurunhampered and
provideeco!<\gicalbenefit.Othervertebrateand invertebratespeciesofpriority
forconservationmay occurnow oratsome timeon theproperty(UCWCS 2005-
2015;UPlF v.2).The Propertypreserveshabitatforspeciesthatmight notnow
be imperiled,orwhich may move intothearea.Priorityhabitatsforconservation
include:mountain riparian,wet meadow, runningwater,standingwater,aspen
forest,mountain shrub("mountainbrush"inthisdocument),and shrubsteppe
("sagebrush- grass"inthisdocument) communities.Otherplantcommunities on I
thepropertyincludeGambel oak and mixed conifer.
The largesizeofthePropertyprovidesvaluetowildlifeand plantsby presenting
avarietyofunfragmentedhabitats.The propertyisalsopartofa largerlandscape
thatincludesoveradditionalthousandsofacresofadjacentprotectedopen space
from SnydervilleBasinintothehighWasatch and UintaMountains,increasingits
valueforlargemammals, mobile species,and specieslesstolerantofdisturbance
by human infrastructure.The Propertyiscontiguous(i.e.,wildlifecantravel
withoutcrossingany roadsordevelopedareas)withtheKimballJunction,Hi Ute
Ranch,and Baker Griswoldconservationeasements,heldby Utah Open Lands,as
wellasthePinebrookopen space;theseopen spacesareinturncontiguouswitha
NationalForestparcel,and otherpendingprotectedopen space.Wasatch Cache &
UintaNationalForest--includingdesignatedwildernessareas--isseparatedfrom
thepropertyby lessthan1.5milesofcurrentlyundeveloped,roadlessopen space
tothesouth.To theeast,theKimball Junctionconservationeasementthatborders
thePropertyisseparatedfrom theSwaner NaturePreserve,which spans
. SnydervilleBasin,by Highway 224..The Swaner NaturePreserveisconnectedto

HistoricalContext
The Propertyreflectsa longprocessofgeologic,climate,vegetation,and wildlife
interactions.Forexample,riparianvegetationinlow-lyingareasstabilizedbanks,
moderatederosion,and contributedorganicmattertostreams.Wood instreams-
functionedtocreatepools,and ashabitatforfishand otherwildlife.The activitiesof
beaversand otherwildlifefurthershapedphysicalstructuressuchasponds and upland
forestsby influencing(sometimescontrolling)processessuchasstreamflow,forest
dynamics,and seeddispersal.Stream channelmigrationhasoccurredfrom ancienttimes
intogeologically-recent,historicalperiods,contributingtothedevelopment ofvalleys,
vegetationcommunities,structuraldiversity,and nutrientcycling.
Aboriginaland IndiantribesincludingUte and Shoshone tribeswere probablypresent
and likelyhuntedand traveledinthearea.
Nearby areaswere settledby non-Indianimmigrantsinthelate1800's.Earlytrappers,
explorers,and immigrantstraveledalongtheroutenow followedby I-80.In1850 this
routeopened forbusinessasatollroadtoSaltLake City;itwas calledthe.GoldenPass
Road (Berrett2007).WilliamHenry Kimball and hiswife,MelissaBurtonCoray
Kimball,ranaPony Expressand stagecoachstopand rentedrooms totravelersnearwhat
isnow calledThreemileCanyons.Silverwas discoveredintheParkCityareaaroundthe
1870's,leadingtoamining boom thatlasteduntilthe1950's,withthelastmine closing
in1978'.Mining,lumbering,and grazingpracticesallcontributedtotheremovaloftrees
from themountainsand otherecologicalchanges.Itisnotknown towhat extenttheplant
and wildlifecommunities on thePropertywere affected,buttheyprobablyexperienced
grazing,logging,and huntingpressuressimilartothoseofnearbyareas.Itislikelythat
grazingoflivestockprobablyoccurredon thePropertyaftersettlementoftheareain
1850.Some timberwas probablyremoved from thehillsidesaroundtheturnofthe20'
century.However, landmanagement foratleast15yearshasnotincludedlivestock
grazing,logging,orotherconsumptiveuses.Forestsand othernaturalplantcommunities
have grown and structuredthemselveswithlittlehuman impactforatleast20 years.The

ECOLOGY
Ecoregion
The PropertyliesintheWasatch and UintaMountains Ecoregion,spanningthemapped
transitionbetween theWasatch Mountain Zone, SemiaridFoothills,and Mountain
Valleys(Woods etal.2001).Although mapping ofLevelIV ecoregionsisdone ata
coarseenough scalethatregionalboundariesarenotexact,thePropertydoesshow
characteristicsofallthreeareas.The Wasatch Mountain Zone ismarked by forested
mountainswithDouglas-firand aspenparkland,withEngelmann spruceand subalpinefir
on steep,north-facingslopes(Woods etal.2001).The SemiaridFoothillsintheareaof
thePropertyaremarked by Maple-oak scrubwithsagebrushand mountain mahogany
(Woods etal.2001).And Mountain Valleysaremarked by terraces,floodplains,alluvial
fans,and hillswithGreatBasinsagebrush(Woods etal.2001).The Propertyoccursin
what Stokes(1986)classifiedastheMiddle Rocky Mountains ofthe Wasatch Range.

Vegetation
Plantcommunities
SagebrushcoversalargeareaoftheProperty,oftendissectingand interweavingintothe
othercommunities.Dominated by bigsagebrush(Artemisiatridentata),thiscommunity
hasothershrubssuchasrubberrabbitbrush(Chrysothamnus nauseosus),Utah
serviceberry(Amelanchierutahensis),bitterbrush(Purshiatridentata),and chokecherry
(Prunusvirginiana)scatteredthroughout.The shrubscreateanoverstoryforforbsand
grassesthatfilltheinterspacebetween shrubs.Grassincludesspeciesofbrome (Bromus
sp.),wheatgrass(Elymus sp.),needlegrass(Stipasp.)and bluegrass(Poa sp.).Introduced
invasiveplantsfoundinthiscommunity include:cheatgrass(Bromus tectorum),Canada
thistle(Cirsiumarvense)and Musk thistle(Carduus nutans).
The mountain brushcommunity includesspeciesadaptedtohillsidesand uplandsofthe
area.Itcanbe dominatedby curl-leafmountain mahogany (Cercocarpusleditolius),
GambePs oak (Quercusgambelii),chokecherry(Prunusvirginiana),ormountain
snowberry(Symphoricarposoreophihts)dependingupon aspect,slopeand elevation.
Standsofbigtoothmaple (Acergrandidentatum)arepresent.Throughout theProperty,
thetransitionbetween thiscommunity and itsborderingcommunities appeartobe
gradualinsome locationsand ratherabruptinothers.
The aspen(Populustremuloides)community isextensiveon theProperty.Aspen trees
createtheoverstoryofacommunity includingsmallertreesand shrubssuchasbigtooth I
maple (Acergrandidentatum),serviceberry(Amelanchieralnifolia)and twinberry
honeysuckle(Lonicerainvolucrate)and forbsand grassessuchasaspenpea (Lathyrus
lanzwertii),borse-nettle(,4gastacheurticifolia),Fendler'smeadowrue (Thalictrum
fendleri),noddingbrome (Bromus anomalus),and orchardgrass(Dactylisglomerata).
Mountain shrubspeciesareinterspersedwithaspenand orconiferinmany places. i
Conifersform acommon-and possiblyincreasing--component ofsome aspen
communitieson theProperty.
I
Conifercommunitieshave adominant over-storyofDouglas fir(Pseudotsugamenziesii), I
whitefir(Abiesconcolor),and/orotherspruceorfirspecies.The interioroftheconifer
community includessmallshrubssuchasOregon grape(Mahonia repens)and mountain- I
lover(Pachystimamyrsinites).
Riparianareastypicallyhave relativelydense,lushvegetationincludingsome species
from adjacentuplandcommunities (e.g.,aspen,bigtoothmaple,whitefir),and some
typicallyriparianspeciesincludingwillow (Salixsp.),rushes(Junt-ussp.),cow parsnip
(Heracleum maximum), and Richardson'sgeranium (Geranium richardsonii).Riparian
meadow communities includegrasses(e.g.,Dactylisglornerata,poa sp.),rushes(Juncus
sp.),and sedges(Carexsp.)inplaces.Invasiveriparianspeciesincludecommon burdock
(Arctiumminus),houndstongue(Cynoglossum officinale)and Canada thistle(Cirsium
arvense).

Wildlife
The Propertyprovideshabitatwithmost oralloftheresourcesneeded by some animals

includingthosethatrequirerelativelyextensivelandscapes,suchasmountain lions(Felis
concolor),and raptors.
Mammals
The Propertyprovidesfood,water,and shelterresourcesformammals; mammals arein
turnintegraltotheecosystem.Smallmammal herbivoresand thosethatfeedon seeds
and fruitsinfluencevegetationand disperseseeds.Some kindsofsmallmammals also
feedon invertebratessuchascricketsand grasshoppers.AllspeciesofUtah batsare
invertivores.The burrows ofsmallmammals provideshelterforotheranimalsand are
thoughttoaidinsoilaerationand waterinfiltration.Forexample,Columbia spottedfrogs
usesmallmammals burrows forshelter.Smallmammals form abaseofpreyspeciesfor
predatorybirds,reptiles,and mammals. Red squirrels(Tamiasciurushudsonicus),Uinta
groundsquirrels(Spermophilusarmatus),and chipmunks (Neotamiasspp.)were
observedattheProperty.Tracksand orfecesthatappearedtobe from coyotes(Canis
latrans)were observed.A redfoxwas observedon theadjacentHi Ute Ranch property;
redfoxarereportedby thelandownertooccurinThreemileCanyon. Itisverylikelythat
bobcats(Lynxrufus)arepresentbecausetheirtrackshave been observednearby.
Beaver-cutwood and otherevidenceofbeaverswas observedon theProperty.Beavers
have helpedovertheyearstoshapeplantcommunities inbothriparianand uplandareas,
and have influencedhydrology.
Largermammals on thePropertycaninfluencesoilstructure,and theirherbivorymay
influencevegetationabundance and species.Largeherbivoresprovidepreyforlarge
predators,and arecommonly culturallydesirableasganieanimalsand foraesthetic
reasons.Mule deer,moose, and elkwere observedon theProperty.Elk and moose are
known towinterinthearea.The Propertyison theedge ofmapped year-roundblack-
bearhabitat(SGlD_Ul00 BlackBear).The Landowner reportsblackbears(Ursus
americanus)and mountain lionsinThreemileCanyon (PaulBuchner,2011,personal
communication).
